Key   customers and COGS 
SJ Semiconductor provides advanced packaging services primarily for leading fabless 
semiconductor companies, foundries and HPC customers. As of 1H25, the top 5 customers 
accounted for 91% of sales, and top 1 customer Huawei accounts for 74% of total revenue.  
SJ Semi's revenue is primarily from China customers; overseas revenue accounted for 6% 
of total revenue in 1H25. 
Manufacturing cost accounted for 50% of COGS in 1H25. Advanced packaging requires 
high levels of clean room, precision, and automation, thus it requires a significant amount 
of advanced  packaging and testing equipment. This equipment and these production lines 
are typically highly expensive, so manufacturing cost usually accounts for a high 
proportion of the operating cost structure.

Shareholding Structure 
As of the 1H26 reporting, the largest shareholders were Wuxi Chanfa  Science &

### Page 46

M
Asia Pacific Insight
46
Technology Venture capital (9.39%), an industry investment fund backed by stated-owned
capital. SJ Semiconductor has no controlling shareholder.
SJ Semiconductor  was originally established in 2014 as a joint venture between
Semiconductor Manufacturing International Corporation (SMIC) and JCET Group,
specializing in advanced semiconductor packaging and testing. In 2021, both companies
sold their stakes in SJ Semi to a consortium of financial investors. The main reason for

these sales was strategic. SMIC stated that the divestment would optimize resource
allocation and allow it to focus on its foundry business. JCET exited its investment to
monetize its holdings and strengthen its balance sheet while concentrating on its core
packaging operations.

Glossary
2.5D: 2.5-Dimensional Integration. Advanced packaging architecture that places multiple
dies, such as AI processors and HBM, side-by-side on an interposer or equivalent high-
density interconnect platform.
3DIC: Three-Dimensional Integrated Circuit. Advanced integration architecture that
vertically stacks dies to increase compute density and shorten interconnects, but with
higher process complexity, cost and yield sensitivity.

ASIC: Application-Specific Integrated Circuit. Chip designed for a specific workload or
application, including custom AI accelerators.
Back-end: Back-end Semiconductor Manufacturing. Semiconductor assembly, packaging
and testing processes performed after wafer fabrication. Advanced packaging is making
these processes increasingly complex and equipment-intensive.
Bumping: Wafer Bumping. Process of forming conductive bumps on a wafer or die to
create electrical connections to another die, interposer or substrate.

C2W: Chip-to-Wafer. 3D integration approach in which individual dies are bonded onto a
wafer, allowing tested dies to be selected before stacking.
Chiplet: A smaller functional die designed to be integrated with other dies within one
package, enabling system performance to scale without relying solely on larger monolithic
chips.
CMP: Chemical Mechanical Planarization. Precision polishing process used to create
extremely flat wafer surfaces, important for advanced 3D integration and hybrid bonding.

CoWoS: Chip-on-Wafer-on-Substrate. Family of 2.5D advanced packaging architectures
that integrates multiple compute and memory dies through high-density interconnects.
CoWoS-L: Chip-on-Wafer-on-Substrate – Local Silicon Interconnect 2.5D architecture
combining an RDL-based interposer with local silicon interconnects, offering greater
scalability and design flexibility for larger AI packages.
CoWoS-S: Chip-on-Wafer-on-Substrate – Silicon Interposer 2.5D architecture using a

full silicon interposer to connect multiple dies.
CP: Chip Probe Electrical testing of individual dies while they remain on the wafer, helping
identify functional dies before downstream packaging.
CPO: Co-Packaged Optics Integration of optical communication components close to
compute or switching chips to increase interconnect bandwidth and reduce power
consumption.
CSP: Cloud Service Provider Large cloud-computing customer that operates data-center

infrastructure and is an important source of demand for AI accelerators and advanced
packaging.
Dicing: Wafer Dicing Process of cutting a processed wafer into individual semiconductor
dies.

### Page 48

M
Asia Pacific Insight
48
ECP: Electrochemical Plating Process that electrochemically deposits conductive metals,
typically copper, for structures including RDL and TSV-related interconnects.
EUV: Extreme Ultraviolet Lithography Advanced lithography technology used in leading-
edge semiconductor wafer fabrication.
Fab: Semiconductor Fabrication Plant Manufacturing facility in which semiconductor
wafers are processed and chips are fabricated.
Fabless: Fabless Semiconductor Company Semiconductor company that designs chips

but outsources wafer manufacturing to foundries.
Fan-in: Fan-in Wafer-Level Packaging Wafer-level packaging architecture in which
package interconnections remain within the footprint of the original die.
Fan-out: Fan-out Packaging Packaging architecture that redistributes connections beyond
the original die footprint, enabling more I/O connections and potentially multi-die
integration.
Fine Pitch: Fine Interconnect Pitch Small spacing between electrical interconnects. Finer

pitch enables higher interconnect density and bandwidth but requires greater
manufacturing precision.
Flip Chip: Flip-Chip Packaging Packaging method in which a die is flipped face-down and
electrically connected through bumps rather than conventional wire bonds.
Foundry: Semiconductor Foundry Manufacturer that fabricates semiconductor wafers for
chip designers and other customers.
Front-end: Front-end Semiconductor Manufacturing Wafer-fabrication processes used

to create transistors and circuitry before semiconductor assembly and packaging.
GPU: Graphics Processing Unit Highly parallel processor widely used for AI workloads
and a key source of demand for 2.5D/3D advanced packaging.
HBM: High-Bandwidth Memory Vertically stacked DRAM positioned close to AI
processors to provide very high memory bandwidth. HBM availability can constrain AI
accelerator and advanced-packaging output.
HBM3E / HBM4 / HBM4E: High-Bandwidth Memory Generations Successive generations

of HBM with increasing bandwidth, capacity and packaging complexity.
Heterogeneous Integration Integration of different types of dies, potentially
manufactured using different process technologies, into a single package or system.
HPC: High-Performance Computing Compute-intensive applications requiring high
processing performance and memory bandwidth, making them major users of advanced
packaging.
Hybrid Bonding: High-density bonding technology that directly connects metal

interconnects and dielectric surfaces between dies or wafers, enabling finer interconnect
pitch for advanced 3D integration.
Interposer: Intermediate layer connecting multiple dies within a package through high-
density electrical interconnects; commonly used in 2.5D packaging.

### Page 49

M
Asia Pacific Insight
Morgan Stanley Research 49
KGD: Known-Good Die: Die that has been tested and confirmed functional before being
incorporated into a multi-die package, helping reduce yield-related losses.
Logic Folding: Huawei's logic-stacking approach that uses vertical integration to shorten
signal paths and increase effective transistor density through system-, circuit- and
packaging-level innovation.
Micro-bump: Very small conductive bump used to create fine-pitch electrical connections

between dies in advanced packages.
NPU: Neural Processing Unit Processor optimized specifically for neural-network and AI
workloads.
OSAT: Outsourced Semiconductor Assembly and Test Semiconductor service provider
specializing in chip assembly, packaging and testing. JCET, SJ Semi, ASE and Amkor are
examples discussed in the report.
Panel-Level Packaging: Packaging approach that processes semiconductor packages on
large rectangular panels rather than conventional circular wafers, potentially improving

manufacturing efficiency and cost at scale.
RDL: Redistribution Layer Fine metal wiring layers that redistribute electrical
connections between dies, bumps and other package components.
Singulation Package / Die Singulation Process of separating individual semiconductor
dies or packages from a processed wafer or panel.
SoC: System-on-Chip Semiconductor integrating multiple system functions, such as
processing, memory control and I/O, onto a single chip.

SoIC: System on Integrated Chips TSMC's 3D integration platform for vertically stacking
semiconductor dies using high-density interconnect technologies.
TCB: Thermo-Compression Bonding Precision bonding process that uses heat and
pressure to connect dies, increasingly important for HBM and advanced 2.5D/3D
packaging.
TSV: Through-Silicon Via Vertical electrical connection passing through silicon, enabling
high-density connections through interposers or vertically stacked dies.

UTR: Utilization Rate Proportion of installed manufacturing capacity that is actually being
used. A key measure of advanced-packaging supply-demand balance.
W2W: Wafer-to-Wafer 3D integration approach in which two complete wafers are aligned
and bonded before being diced into individual devices.
Wafer Thinning: Wafer Thinning / Grinding Process of reducing wafer thickness, enabling
thinner dies and tighter vertical stacking in HBM and 3D packages.

WLCSP: Wafer-Level Chip-Scale Package Wafer-level packaging in which the completed
package is approximately the same size as the semiconductor die.
WLPL Wafer-Level Packaging Packaging process performed while semiconductor dies
remain part of the wafer rather than after individual die singulation.
XDFOI: XDFOI Advanced Packaging Platform JCET's advanced packaging platform used

### Page 50

M
Asia Pacific Insight
50
for high-density and heterogeneous integration applications.

Analyst Stock Ratings
Overweight (O). The stock's total return is expected to exceed the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next
12-18 months.
Equal-weight (E). The stock's total return is expected to be in line with the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over

the next 12-18 months.
Not-Rated (NR). Currently the analyst does not have adequate conviction about the stock's total return relative to the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage
universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next 12-18 months.
Underweight (U). The stock's total return is expected to be below the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next

12-18 months.

below.

2026.09.10 
洪有辰 C00270@ibfs.com.tw 
8103.TT 瀚荃 
 瀚海智聯，荃新超越

[財務資訊更新，座談會更新]   
   公司簡介 
瀚荃為全球知名的精密電子連接器、軟性排線(FFC)與線纜組件製造大廠，公司以自有
品牌 CviLux 行銷全球，近年成功從傳統的個人電腦零組件廠商，轉型為跨足 AI 伺服
器 、 低軌衛星 、 工業控制 、 車用電子等高附加價值領域的關鍵零組件解決方案供應商 。 
投資評等/目標價 重點摘要 
買進 158 
【 前 次 投 資 建 議 】區 間 操 作 ，138 
【大盤指數/股價】47183 / 113

➢ 2Q26 北美 CSP 廠拉貨動能強勁，高毛利產品占比急增 
2Q26 營收 10.97 億元，QoQ+20.6%，YoY+29%，其中佔比最高的伺
服器/網通與佔比次高的工業電子營收 YoY 分別為+82%與+19%；1Q26
貴金屬價格較佳，使 2Q26 的下腳料處分金額減少，加上正值新產品導
入初期，毛利率 37%，QoQ-1ppts，YoY-0.5ppts；儘管如此，整體費
用率下滑至 20.8%，帶動稅後 EPS 上升至 1.58 元，QoQ+36.2%，
YoY+267.4%。 
➢ 3Q26 成長動能來自伺服器/網通與工業領域 
展望 3Q26，瀚荃成長動能主要來自 AI 伺服器電源連接器及工業自動化
需求。受惠高電流端子技術、客製化提升及與台系電源廠協同設計，伺
服器/網通營收預估 YoY+58.6%；工業領域受智慧倉儲及歐美製造回流
帶動，營收YoY+34.5%，筆電與汽車則持穩。預估3Q26 營收 11.48 億
元 ， 伺服器/網通及工業占比 49%、18%， 產品組合優化帶動毛利率升至
38.2%，QoQ+1.2ppts，EPS 上升至 2.25 元，QoQ+42.6%，YoY+68.2%。 
➢ AI 電源成長動能強，800V HVDC 帶動板端連接器需求續升 
AI 伺服器功耗提升使傳統 AC 架構面臨轉換效率低及熱損耗高等瓶頸，
800V HVDC將逐步普及 ，PSU、BBU、CBU 等電力設備亦將拆分為 Power 
Sidecar，以釋放機櫃空間。瀚荃主導 DC 板端連接器，受惠次世代伺服
器電力設備連接器需求增加，預估 2026 年網通/伺服器營收 16.31 億
元，YoY+35%，占總營收 38%；整體營收 43.39 億元，YoY+29.3%，
毛利率 38%、營益率 16.6%，稀釋後 EPS 7.37 元，YoY+56%。 
➢ 預估瀚荃2027 年 EPS 11.5 元，首次給予買進評等，目標價158 元 
投研部預估瀚荃 2027 年營收 55.51 億元 ，YoY+27.9%； 毛利率40.4%，
YoY+2.5ppts； 營業利益率20.45%，YoY+3.9ppts； 稅後淨利率16.25%，
YoY+3ppts；稅後 EPS 11.5 元。評價方面，觀察過去 5 年 PER 區間大
部分在 10X~22X，目前評價位於區間下緣的 10.4X，考量瀚荃的板端電
源連接器在新一代 AI 伺服器電源架構中具備關鍵地位 ， 再加上旗下客戶
對工業自動化的需求強勁 ， 因此可望享有網通/伺服器與工業領域的雙成
長動能，故給予首次買進投資建議，目標價 158 元(2026F PER 14X)。

8103 瀚荃 加權指數

### Page 2

- 2 -

個股報告

公司簡介 
瀚荃成立於 1990 年，總部位於新北市淡水區，為全球知名的精密電子連接器、軟性
排線(FFC)與線纜組件製造大廠，公司以自有品牌 CviLux 行銷全球，近年成功從傳統
的個人電腦零組件廠商，轉型為跨足 AI 伺服器、低軌衛星、工業控制、車用電子等高
附加價值領域的關鍵零組件解決方案供應商。在海外佈局方面，公司的廠區包含中國
東莞、蘇州、重慶等，以及在東南亞的越南與寮國等地，目前公司將原定於蘇州廠生
產的筆電產能，逐步移往重慶廠生產，並規劃旗下的泰國廠於 2026 年底進行投產。
瀚荃在 2026 的營收結構當中，預估伺服器/網通比重 38%、筆電 18%、工業 17%、
汽車電子 3%、其他 14%。 
圖 1：瀚荃產品營收比重與投研部預估

資料來源：瀚荃年報、國票投顧預估 
2Q26 北美 CSP 廠拉貨動能強勁，高毛利產品佔比急增 
2Q26 營收 10.97 億元，QoQ+20.6%，YoY+29%，其中佔比最高(達 48%)的伺服器
/網通與佔比次高(達 20%)的工業電子營收 YoY 分別為+82%與+19%；毛利率 37%，
QoQ-1ppts，YoY-0.5ppts，因 1Q26 貴金屬價格較佳，使 2Q26 的下腳料處分金額
相較 1Q26 減少約 15%，再加上正值新產品導入初期，毛利率出現短暫下滑所致；營
業利益率 16.2%，QoQ-2ppts，YoY-0.3ppts，下滑原因來自 1Q26 支付收購子公司
全宇昕(6651)相關顧問費以及認列庫藏股轉讓員工費用 ， 使營業費用增加 ； 儘管如此 ，
整體費用率下滑至 20.8%，QoQ-3ppts，YoY-0.2ppts，帶動稅後 EPS 上升至 1.58
元，QoQ+36.2%，YoY+267.4%。 
2H26 成長動能來自伺服器/網通與工業領域 
展望 3Q26， 瀚荃最大的成長動能仍將來自AI 伺服器電源連接器與工業自動化相關需
求。公司具備高電流應用的特殊端子結構技術與專利，針對 DC 端所設計的板端連接
器可望受惠於客製化增加與複雜度上升，並持續透過與多家台系電源企業協同設計而
間接切入輝達與北美 CSP 大廠供應鏈，因此投研部預估相關產品的 ASP 上升將使伺
服器/網通在 3Q26 的營收 YoY+58.6%；工業領域包含自動控制、風電與醫療等少量
多樣的產品，預計將持續受惠於智慧倉儲及歐美製造業回流帶動的自動化需求，相關
營收 YoY+34.5%。致於筆電與汽車業務的表現則相對平穩。投研部預估 3Q26 營收
為 11.48 億元，其中伺服器/網通佔比 49%，工業領域佔比為 18%；由於伺服器/網通
與工業領域的毛利率偏高，預估分別為 50%與 45%，在產品組合優化的情況下，投
研部預估 3Q26 毛利率為 38.2%，QoQ+1.2ppts，預估將帶動稅後 EPS 由 2Q26 的
1.58 元上升至 3Q26 的 2.25 元，QoQ+42.6%，YoY+68.2%。

### Page 3

- 3 -

個股報告 
AI 電源成長動能強，800V HVDC 帶動板端連接器需求持續上升 
圖 2：AI 伺服器 800V DC 電源架構

資料來源：瀚荃法說會簡報、Digitimes 
當前部分 AI 伺服器供電仍採用 AC 電源架構，但隨著伺服器功耗突破千瓦級，傳統
AC 配電系統開始浮現轉換效率偏低與熱耗損攀升的技術瓶頸，而 800V HVDC( 高壓
直流電源)未來將逐漸普及，電流在進入白空間的機櫃後直接以 DC 形式傳送，以減少
AC 與 DC 之間的轉換次數，同時 PSU(電源供應器)、BBU(電池備援單元)與 CBU(電
容備援單元)等電力設備會逐漸從 Compute Rack 拆分成獨立的 Power Sidecar ，並
放置於背板或與匯流排連接，以釋出更多伺服器空間放置更多數量的其他電子元件。
連接器在傳統的伺服器內多為標準品， AC 市電端的連接器與線材由國外大廠如 TE 
Connectivity(TEL.US)與 Amphenol(APH.US)主導，但 DC 板端則由台廠的瀚荃所主
導，尤其是在次世代技術中，單一機櫃內的 PSU、BBU 與 CBU 等所需的板端連接器
數量將明顯提升，因此投研部預估公司 2026 年的網通/伺服器營收為 16.31 億元，
YoY+35%，佔總營收 38%，2026 年預估營收 43.39 億元，YoY+29.3%；因產品組
合 優 化 且 中 國 華 南 地 區 廠 房 產 線 整 合 的 情 況 下 ， 預 估 毛 利 率 將 上 升 至38% ，
YoY+0.6ppts； 營業利益率16.6%，YoY+3.2ppts； 稅後淨利率13.3%，YoY+3.2ppts；
稀釋後 EPS 7.37 元，YoY+87.1%。 
圖 3：瀚荃產品營收比重與投研部預估

資料來源：瀚荃法說會簡報、Digitimes

給予首次買進投資建議，目標價 158 元(2026F PER 14X)，YoY+56%。

## Document 194: A股三大股指集体收跌，农业种植掀涨停潮，航运、创新药逆势大涨，贵金属齐跌，港股AI大模型股双雄集体反弹

### Page 1

盘⾯上，个股涨跌互现，全市场超 2800 只个股下跌。今⽇成交 1.84 万亿。沪深两市成交额
1.82 万亿，较上⼀个交易⽇缩量 160 亿。板块⽅⾯，覆铜板、⻩⾦、稀⼟、电⼒、⽯化板块
表现低迷，创新药、农业股全天强势，炒作资⾦午后流向海运、发电设备、汽⻋板块。
摘要：
在美联储偏鹰加息的冲击下， A 股和港股市场⻛险情绪承压，但 A 股市场结构性主线清晰：农业
种植与粮⻝概念逆势掀涨停潮，受《数字乡村⾼质量发展⾏动计划（ 2026—2030 年）》印发、
⽟⽶制种⾯积下降、厄尔尼诺强化粮价上⾏预期三重催化，前期强势的⻩⾦股则集体重挫。港
股 AI ⼤模型股反弹，智谱盘中涨 7% ，得益于智谱 9 ⽉ 16 ⽇表⽰，有信⼼将年末 ARR （年度经常
性收⼊）指引从 24 亿美元上调⾄ 30 亿美元。
美联储三年多来⾸次加息的 " 靴⼦ " 落地之后， 9 ⽉ 17 ⽇， A 股全天低开震荡、冲⾼回落，三⼤股
指集体收跌。微盘股⾛势较强，收涨超 1% 。板块⽅⾯，⽟⽶、种植业与林业、粮⻝概念、航运

港⼝、教育、⽣物制品、汽⻋整⻋涨幅居前；贵⾦属领跌，元件、 MLCC 概念、铜箔 / 覆铜板、
油⽓开采及服务、⼩⾦属、稀⼟、有⾊等前期强势赛道集体回调。
港股低开低⾛，恒指、恒科指早盘双双跌超 1% ，午后延续跌势，科⽹股多数下挫，美团、阿
⾥、腾讯、⽹易、百度等⻬跌， AI ⼤模型股逆势反弹，智谱⼀度涨 7% ， MINIMAX ⼤涨 7% 。债
市⽅⾯，国债期货震荡上涨。商品⽅⾯，国内商品期货多数下跌。
A 股：截⾄收盘，沪指跌 0.41% ，深成指跌 0.33% ，创业板指跌 0.40% 。

### Page 4

核⼼逻辑在于地缘冲突对运价的超预期推升。中信建投指出，本周国际油运市场⼤幅上涨 ——
美伊爆发冲突以来最⼤规模相互袭击，沙特东西原油管道遇袭关闭，中东原油海陆外运通道同
时中断， TD3C 中东 — 中国航线 TCE 逼近百万美元 / 天关⼝；原油轮各航线同步重估，美湾、西⾮
⼤幅跟涨，成品油轮显著⾛强， LR ⻓程航线收益翻倍。
华源证券持相似观点，认为当前美伊冲突通过谈判实现永久解决的预期正在明显减弱，⻛险溢
价持续上升，短期 VLCC 有望 " 货量与运价共振 " ，运价或持续⾛⾼，同时产业正在逐步定价运⼒
紧张的持续性，期租租⾦有望同步⾛强。
业绩端同样印证⾼景⽓。招商轮船上半年实现营业收⼊ 196.51 亿元，同⽐增⻓ 56.15% ，归⺟净
利润 69.6 亿元，同⽐增⻓ 227.57% ；其中油轮运输业务营收 95.83 亿元，同⽐增⻓ 115.66% ，净
利润 61.89 亿元，同⽐增⻓ 378.62% 。公司董秘在半年报业绩说明会上表⽰，四季度⾼景⽓已基
本确认，资产市场与中⻓期运费市场已在定价 2027 年及之后的景⽓度。

中银证券认为，中⻓期看全球能源贸易格局重塑加速，⼤西洋⾄亚洲⻓航线占⽐提升，将对全
球油运运⼒配置产⽣深远影响，建议关注中东局势下的油运板块机会，包括招商轮船、中远海
能、招商南油等。
创新药 /CRO ：创新药出海破 1200 亿美元， AI 制药催化持续发酵
创新药与 CRO 概念连续第⼆⽇⾛强，成为资⾦的主要避⻛港。⾦⽯亚药开盘 3 分钟即 20cm 直线
涨停，收 12.49 元涨 19.98% ；百花医药涨停；南华⽣物涨停；近岸蛋⽩盘中触及 20cm 涨停，收
盘涨 18.75% 。万邦医药、义翘神州、百普赛斯等涨幅靠前。

催化来⾃两个维度：
其⼀，创新药出海加速。国家药监局在 9 ⽉ 14 ⽇举⾏的 2026 年全国药品安全宣传周启动仪式上披

⻓ 36% ；⾸付款规模超百亿美元，呈现 " 单笔平均交易⾦额稳步增⻓、⾸付款⾦额与占⽐双双提
升、⼤额交易频频落地 " 的特点。 BD 交易的持续活跃使药企研发资⾦趋于充裕，作为产业链 " 卖
⽔⼈ " 的 CRO 企业在⼿订单呈现 " 量价⻬升 " 态势。

款 AI 辅助创新药获批上市， AI 制药正从概念验证⾛向产业化落地。此外， 9 ⽉ 12—15 ⽇世界肺癌

### Page 7

兴业证券认为，创新药全球竞争⼒持续加强，板块驱动⼒正由估值向 " 业绩 + 全球化兑现 " 切换；

润增速接近上⼀轮周期⾼点，⾏业正从 " 短期修复 " 转向 " 成⻓定价 " 。
农业种植 / 粮⻝概念：政策、去库存、厄尔尼诺三重共振，逆势掀涨停潮
农业种植是当⽇最热点板块，也是唯⼀的持续性主线。万向德农 2 连板；⾦健⽶业涨停；敦煌种
业涨停，公司上半年净利润同⽐预增 157.1% ；华英农业涨停。神农种业涨 8.62% ，⾦丹科技涨
14.10% ，秋乐种业、国投丰乐等涨幅居前。
值得注意的是，⾃ 8 ⽉ 17 ⽇起的最近⼀个⽉内，⾦健⽶业累计收获九涨停，股价从 6 元 / 股左右⼀
度攀⾄ 15.07 元 / 股的阶段⾼位，最新股价 13.02 元 / 股。
催化逻辑可拆为三条链：
⼤会（ WCLC ）在⾸尔举⾏，中国研究⼊选⼝头报告 19 项、⼩型⼝头报告 45 项，刷新历史纪

录，为板块提供密集的数据催化。
其⼀，政策端落地。中央⽹信办、农业农村部、⼯信部联合印发《数字乡村⾼质量发展⾏动计划
（ 2026—2030 年）》，提出强化农业科技创新与数字化赋能，为种业、智慧农业提供中⻓期政策
预期。
其⼆，供给端去库存。财信证券研报指出， 2026 年⽟⽶制种⾯积同⽐下降 7% ，叠加⾼温⼲旱影
响单产，⾏业库存压⼒边际缓解；国投证券认为，全球⽟⽶库销⽐处于历史低位，厄尔尼诺预警
强化粮价中⻓期上⾏预期。

安全底线。

## Document 215: Eight Reasons Why the Fed Should Raise Rates (2026-09-14)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Eight Reasons Why the Fed Should Raise Rates (2026-09-14).pdf
Report source: Unknown
Date: 未知日期
Pages: 2
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

The bank
for a changing
world
3
economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoWeek 26.33 / 14 September 2026EDITORIAL
CHART 1
THE EIGHT REASONS WHY THE FED SHOULD RAISE RATES
1 U.S. productivity gains are significant but attributing them to the AI boom is
premature: see our Chart of the Week from May 27, 2026, United States: Labour
productivity growth set to continue.
The FOMC meeting on September 15–16 is expected to mark a turning point with the Fed’s first rate hike since May 2023.

While there may have been economic reasons to hold off and maintain the status quo until now (some negative signals
on the employment front and some encouraging ones on the inflation front), the conditions for a necessary recalibration
now appear to be in place.
Between the start of the year and September 11, out of a sample
of nearly 90 central banks worldwide, 25 lowered their policy rates
(an average cumulative decrease of 85 basis points), 25 raised them

(an average cumulative increase of 75 basis points), and 36 left them
unchanged. The U.S. Federal Reserve is part of this status quo group,
while the ECB is among the central banks that have raised their rates
(twice). Which is the odd one, the Fed or the ECB? At first glance, the
Fed’s status quo seems harder to explain than the ECB’s rate hike:
U.S. growth and inflation are significantly higher than Eurozone me -
trics. Why would this situation warrant a rate hike in the latter case but

not (yet) in the former? In short, because the Fed has a dual mandate
and, until recently, its focus has been more on the maximum employ -
ment component than on price stability.
Will the Fed continue to diverge from its peers this week, given that
the ECB implemented another rate hike on September 10 th and that
further tightening by the BoJ (September 17 th–18th) is also widely an -
ticipated? And while a status quo from the BoE (which will also meet

on September 17th) is expected, the vote is likely to be close (6-3 ac -
cording to our forecasts, possibly 6-4) and the tone should be hawkish.
In our new base case scenario, we believe the Fed will join the ranks
of the hikers this week: it would no longer wait until December 2026
to begin the (short) cycle of (three) rate hikes (25 bp each) that we
have been anticipating for several months. However, the outcome of
the September 15–16 FOMC meeting remains somewhat uncertain: we

believe the conditions are in place for a monetary policy adjustment.
But will the Fed share the same view?
WHAT HAS HELD THE FED BACK FROM RAISING RATES SO FAR? SOME SIGNS
OF WEAKNESS IN THE LABOUR MARKET AND THE K-SHAPED NATURE OF
GROWTH
This latter is running at a relatively high pace (an average of 2.2% year-
over-year since Q1 2025, double the Eurozone growth rate) but rests
on narrow foundations that are largely dependent on the AI boom. It is

this sector that is driving a large portion of non-residential investment
(with, conversely, equally significant imports). And it is the wealth ef -
fects stemming from the sharp rise in stock market indices—driven by
tech stocks—that largely underpin consumption among the wealthiest
households, while consumption among lower-income households is
struggling, constrained, and affected by the high cost of living.
Strong on the surface but more fragile beneath: this characteristic of

U.S. growth has likely contributed, thus far, to tipping the scales in fa-
vor of the monetary status quo. The aim was to avoid the risk of stalling
the AI engine and exacerbating the financial difficulties of lower-inco -
me households. An idea has also circulated: that this strong growth,
driven by productivity gains (supposedly resulting from the deploy -
ment of AI1), might be disinflationary rather than inflationary.
This theory has not, for the time being, been fully validated by the data:

on the contrary, the AI boom appears to be rather inflationary in the
short term, which should lead the Fed to reconsider its status quo.
The labour market, like economic growth, has both a positive side and a
more worrisome one. The first is characterized by a low unemployment
rate, downwardly oriented over the past few months, a trend which is
expected to continue. The second stems from the volatility and relative
weakness of job gains through July, the downward pressures on labour

supply, and the uncomfortable balance of a situation combining low firing
and low hiring. The Fed has been more sensitive to these vulnerabilities,
in line with its dual mandate.
HIGH INFLATION — THOUGH TRENDING DOWNWARD ACCORDING TO CER-
TAIN ALTERNATIVE MEASURES — AND ANCHORED INFLATION EXPECTA-
TIONS HAVE ALSO HELPED THE FED ADOPT A WAIT-AND-SEE APPROACH.
U.S. inflation is high and has been well above the 2% target for now
six years, according to the most widely used metrics; while alternative

measures are closer to the target (see Chart 1). The trend, drivers, and
composition of inflation also matter. As for its trend, data from June
and July showed a slight downward trend, an encouraging sign further
supported by the stability (no increase) seen in August (3.4% year-on-
year for the headline CPI, with core inflation even edging down slightly
from 2.5% to 2.4% y/y).

The bank
for a changing

On the other hand, if we compare inflation with the trend in unit labor
costs—a key driver, as Richard Clarida (former Fed vice chair) 2 points
out—it clearly appears to be trending downward, with further disinfla-
tion on the horizon (see Chart 2).
Finally, in terms of composition, current inflationary pressures stem
largely from the energy supply shock caused by the conflict in Iran.

Such a development does not require an immediate response from
the central bank 3, as long as inflation expectations remain anchored
(which is currently the case) and provided that the inflation spike re -
mains transitory, does not become widespread, and does not trigger
second-round effects (which cannot be taken entirely for granted over
the next few months, given persistent upward pressure on energy
prices combined with resilient U.S. growth).

WHY IS THE MONETARY STATUS QUO NO LONGER TENABLE? THERE ARE AT
LEAST SEVEN REASONS
A combination of factors justifies the Fed raising its policy rates, star -
ting in September. From our perspective, it should have already done
so, given the economic and inflation data.
First, the current environment is completely different and significantly
more favorable than the one that prevailed at the time of the three
insurance rate cuts in late 2025. Undoing this easing alone is justified

today.
Second, the real GDP growth rate is well above its potential pace.
Our 3rd, 4th and 5th points are the following: unemployment is trending
downward, inflation is well above target, and there is a risk that infla-
tion expectations could become unanchored.
Sixth, the balance of risks between the two components of the Fed’s
mandate has shifted: the easing of employment concerns brings the
upward risks to inflation to the forefront.
Seventh, U.S. monetary policy is likely too accommodative — to a de -

gree difficult to assess precisely, given the inability to accurately esti -
mate the neutral rate (which, according to our estimates, falls within a
range of 3.25% to 4.25% in nominal terms).
2 See this Financial Times article: The Key Inflation Signal for Investors (August 19, 2026).
3 See this BIS paper for a recent overview of the debate: Energy Shocks and Inflation: Challenges for Monetary Policy, August 5, 2026.
4 65 months of core PCE inflation overshooting the 2% target.

But it is clear that current high productivity gains are pushing it higher.
A recalibration of monetary policy therefore appears necessary to bring
the Fed Funds target (currently 3.50–3.75%) at least toward the upper
end of our estimate of the neutral rate, or even into restrictive territory.
EIGHTH, THE FED MUST BACK UP ITS WORDS WITH ACTION
Regarding the outcome of the September 15–16 FOMC meeting, Ke -
vin Warsh has not shown willingness to act upon the latest data. He

wants a Fed that is less data-dependent and more guided by under -
lying trends. It is understandable and this shift could happen in the fu-
ture, following the conclusions of the task forces. However, aside from
the fact that Kevin Warsh is not the sole decision-maker, the latest
data for August points towards a rate hike. This is clear in light of the
strong rebound in nonfarm payroll employment. It is also clear regar -
ding inflation, as the monthly change (seasonally adjusted) in the core

CPI (+0.29%) exceeds the trigger threshold we had identified (a “big”
0.2%). In fact, the very persistence of high inflation4, combined with an
economic environment (both domestic and international) that remains
inflationary, argues for a rise in the Fed Funds target.
It would also be a matter of Kevin Warsh walking the talk to reinforce
the credibility of his remarks at Jackson Hole regarding the renewed
firmness of his commitment to ensure price stability. And it would also

be a matter of not adding to the tension and nervousness already pre-
sent in the bond markets, regardless of their origin. If the Fed takes the
markets’ signal into account — as Kevin Warsh seems inclined to do
when he considers that the rise in long-term rates is doing part of the
central bank’s job and when he highlights the reassuring anchoring of
their inflation expectations — then it must raise its policy rates without
further delay. Not only do economic conditions require it, but it is also

a matter of risk management.
Hélène Baudchon
helene.baudchon@bnpparibas.com

## Document 259: Online betting platforms_ What’s the money line_ Online betting adoption is rising, especially among Gen Z and Millennials, while participants show lower financial cushion. 2 min r

Source PDF: all report pdf/Online betting platforms_ What’s the money line_ Online betting adoption is rising, especially among Gen Z and Millennials, while participants show lower financial cushion. 2 min read.pdf
Report source: Unknown
Date: 未知日期
Pages: 9
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

13014118     1

Economy 
Online betting platforms: What’s the money line? 
01 September 2026

Key takeaways 
• Online betting adoption is accelerating. According to Bank of America consumer account data, the number of online betting 
users rose sharply this summer, with first-time users in June and July more than triple January levels. Activity is concentrated 
among younger consumers, with Gen Z and Millennials accounting for 88% of online betting activity in July. 
• Based on observed inflows and outflows to these platforms, customer inflows averaged less than three-quarters of outflows 
during the period analyzed. While these figures may not capture winnings that remain on platforms, they suggest online betting 
is not currently functioning as a meaningful source of income for most bettors. 
• In 2026, the median deposit account balance of households that participated in online betting was 59% of households that did 
not. With prediction markets rapidly expanding alongside traditional sports betting, this has prompted some to blur the lines 
between entertainment and investment, according to a Bank of America proprietary survey.

Place your bets – increasingly online 
Increasingly, online betting appears to be gaining adoption. In this analysis, we identify online betting payments (automated 
clearing house (ACH), debit card and credit card) to and from companies that offer online betting platforms – namely prediction 
markets and sports betting. Note that this excludes online social gambling from typical casinos or other gaming platforms. See 
Methodology for more details. 
According to Bank of America customer account data, around 5% of customers participated in online betting in July. Yet 
consumer adoption of online betting platforms has been rising rapidly and was up 40% in July compared to the start of the year 
(Exhibit 1). Perhaps more notably, the number of first-time (i.e. someone with a first-time payment to one of these platforms) 
online betting users jumped more than 3x that of January users in June and July. See Methodology for first-time users. 
Exhibit 1: In June and July, the number of first-time online betting users jumped around 3x that of the start of the year 
Online betting users (number of customers, index January 2026 = 100, monthly)

Source: Bank of America internal data

Sports calendar drives spending 
Some of the June/July peak was likely driven in part by promotions and buzz related to prediction markets as well as the FIFA 
World Cup 2026™ (read more on the economic impact of the World Cup in August’s On the ball). Our analysis suggests online 
betting activity appears quite volatile and predominantly tied to the timing of major sporting events.  
For example, the growth in first-time users is apparent in both the basketball and football seasons (both college and professional 
leagues), with the latter having seen 22% year-over-year (YoY) growth in the 2025 season (Exhibit 2). Football season is defined 
as September through February and therefore overlaps with other major league sports such as baseball and hockey. 
According to a recent CivicScience survey, over a third of those who partake in online sports betting did so weekly and almost a 
quarter placed bets daily (Exhibit 3). Women were less likely to report doing so frequently (i.e. daily or weekly) compared to men. 
Exhibit 2: First-time online betting users grew 22% YoY in the 2025

Gen Z doubles down 
Who is placing the bets? According to Bank of America consumer account data, across income terciles, online betting activity is 
relatively evenly split, although lower-income households comprised a slightly larger share of online betting activity than other 
income groups (Exhibit 4). 
However, by generation, online betting activity within Bank of America consumer account data is heavily skewed towards Gen Z 
and Millennials. In July, they comprised 88% of online betting activity (Exhibit 5). Notably, until June, Millennials accounted for a 
larger share of activity, suggesting Gen Z initiated a turning point in the composition of online bettors this summer.

I NSTI TUTE

The concentration of betting activity among Gen Z may reflect broader shifts toward app-based financial and entertainment 
experiences (read more on Gen Z behavior in August’s Gen Z reality check). Younger consumers have generally been quicker to 
adopt emerging digital platforms, including crypto, buy now pay later (BNPL) products and online marketplaces (read more on 
online marketplace adoption in April’s Secondhand fashion piece). 
Are the odds in your favor? 
With more consumers increasing their online betting activity, are they also bringing in extra income? Using Bank of America 
payments data, we measure the inflow-to-outflow payment ratio of customers placing bets. It is important to note that we can 
only see money deposited to and from these platforms each month and therefore may not capture winnings that remain on 
betting platforms and have not yet been withdrawn.  
However, our analysis found the online betting cash recovery ratio has remained below 1, with total inflows less than three-
quarters of total outflows on average for the duration of the series (Exhibit 6). In other words, customers typically recover less 
than 75 cents for every dollar transferred to online betting platforms.  
Exhibit 6: The online betting cash recovery ratio has remained below 1, with total inflows less than three-quarters that of total outflows on average 
for the duration of the series 
Online betting cash recovery ratio (monthly, 2026)

Source: Bank of America internal data 
Note: A ratio less than 1 means inflow was less than outflow. These estimates should be interpreted as observed inflows and outflows to betting platforms rather than realized profitability, since winnings 
retained on-platform are not fully observable. 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Does Gen Z see online betting as a source of income? 
Although Gen Z participants had the highest online betting cash recovery ratio across income groups, they still fell well short of 
breaking even (Exhibit 7). Despite seemingly recovering more than older generations, total inflows remained substantially below 
total outflows, suggesting that online betting is not a reliable or constant source of income. 
Exhibit 7: While Gen Z still recovers less of what they spend, older generations have the lowest cash recovery ratio

20% consider sports betting as a type of investment 
Despite the lack of profitability, investing and betting appears increasingly blurred for some consumers. Prediction markets, 
crypto assets, retail trading and sports betting all share common features such as community participation and real-time pricing. 
According to a Bank of America proprietary survey, 20% of respondents consider sports betting as a type of investment, 
although more consider it not a form of investing (Exhibit 8). Gen Z is twice as likely to consider sports betting as a form of 
investing; however, across all generations, buying event contracts on prediction markets was more likely to be considered an 
investment than sports betting.  
Exhibit 8: Gen Z is more likely to consider buying event contracts on prediction markets or sports betting as a non-traditional form of investing 
Classifications of investing activities (% of respondents by generation)

Source: Bank of America proprietary survey 
Note: Survey conducted March 24-31, 2026 across 2,351 respondents. 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

The financial spread widens 
Does the financial health playbook look different between households who gamble versus those who don’t? Yes, although some 
of this comes down to demographics. 
Following the 2018 Murphy v. NCAA Supreme Court decision opening the door for states to legalize sports betting, according to 
a working paper from the National Bureau of Economic Research, as legalization went into effect, sports betting spread quickly, 
with both the number of participants and frequency of bets increasing over time. This increase did not displace other gambling 
or consumption but significantly reduced savings, as risky bets crowd out positive expected value investments.1  
That same paper found these effects were concentrated among financially constrained households. Bank of America customer 
deposit account data found that of those households that participate in online betting in 2026, their median deposit account 
balances were only 59% of those who did not (Exhibit 9).  
In addition to having lower median deposit balances, households with online betting activity have stronger total card spending 
growth excluding online betting (Exhibit 10). These households also had stronger spending growth in July across both 
discretionary and necessity items, though this could also be because younger and lower-income households spending growth has 
strengthened recently (read more on this in the August Consumer Checkpoint).

1 Baker, S.R., Balthrop, J., Johnson, M.J., Kotter, J.D., & Pisciotta, K. (2024, November). Gambling Away Stability: Sports Betting’s Impact on Vulnerable 
Households. National Bureau of Economic Research.

BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

Policymakers enter the game with a possible new set of rules 
While sports betting remains the dominant area within online betting, broader prediction markets appear to be attracting 
increasing consumer attention too. According to BofA Global Public Policy, wagers have been placed (or markets have been 
created) on almost everything: whether a political speaker will drink water on camera during a speech, the total flu cases 
predicted for the year, etc. These features have raised the question of whether such instruments function more like financial 
contracts or resemble forms of wagering under state law.  
Prediction markets operate by listing standardized contracts – often referred to as event contracts – that pay out based on 
whether a specified outcome occurs (Exhibit 11). According to BofA Global Public Policy, the Commodities Futures Trading 
Commission (CFTC) argues that certain prediction market event contracts, when traded on federally regulated exchanges, 
constitute derivatives (classified as commodity interests under the Commodity Exchange Act (CEA)) and are therefore subject to 
federal oversight. 
Exhibit 11: Prediction markets vary from both sports betting and other financial contracts 
Comparison of prediction markets with futures and swaps 
Dimension Futures Swaps Prediction Markets 
Core purpose Hedge or manage exposure to future 
price movements 
Manage exposure to variability in a defined 
reference 
Aggregate views about whether a specified 
event will occur

CFTC creates rulemaking around incentive programs 
The CFTC recently implemented rulemaking around perpetual futures contracts (i.e. speculating an asset's price with no 
expiration date) and put out an advisory regarding incentive programs for event contract trading. Within this, the CFTC warned 
against sweepstakes-like or randomized reward programs, or prizes based on pure chance. 
Growing concerns about the rapid rise of prediction markets have prompted lawmakers from both parties and chambers of 
Congress to introduce several bills aimed at establishing clearer federal rules, particularly around consumer protections. At the 
same time, many states and tribal regulators argue that some event contracts, especially those tied to sports and entertainment, 
closely resemble traditional gambling and should remain subject to existing state gaming, licensing and consumer protection 
laws. 
According to BofA Global Research, the CFTC is aiming to make clearer distinctions between sports books and prediction 
markets highlighted by recent rule proposals and advisories regarding in-house market making, incentive programs and casino 
style odds.

Methodology 
Selected Bank of America transaction data is used to inform the macroeconomic views expressed in this report and should be considered in the 
context of other economic indicators and publicly available information. In certain instances, the data may provide directional and/or predictive 
value. The data used is not comprehensive; it is based on aggregated and anonymized selections of Bank of America data and may reflect a 
degree of selection bias and limitations on the data available. 
Bank of America credit/debit card spending per household includes spending from active US households only. Only consumer card holders 
making a minimum of five transactions a month are included in the dataset. Spending from corporate cards is excluded. Data regarding 
merchants who receive payments are identified and classified by the Merchant Categorization Code (MCC) defined by financial services 
companies. The data are mapped using proprietary methods from the MCCs to the North American Industry Classification System (NAICS), 
which is also used by the Census Bureau, in order to classify spending data by subsector. Spending data may also be classified by other 
proprietary methods not using MCCs.  
If applicable, the consumer deposit data based on Bank of America internal data is derived by anonymizing and aggregating data from Bank of 
America consumer deposit accounts in the US and analyzing that data at a highly aggregated level.  
If applicable, any payments data represents aggregated spend from US Retail, Preferred, Small Business and Wealth Management clients with a 
deposit account or credit card. Any reference to aggregated AI spend include total credit card, debit card, ACH, or bill pay.  
Online betting transactions conducted through Bank of America debit and credit card or ACH channels and online betting platforms are 
identified using industry-researched merchant names. Note that the scope of this series is limited to predominantly online sports betting, horse 
racing, and prediction markets. 
First-time users are defined as customers making their first observed transaction in the category during the particular month, based on 
transaction records dating back to 2015. 
Football season (defined as September through February) was selected as a measure as this captures a sizeable amount of online betting 
activity. 
The measures in this report reflect customer payment flows to and from identified online betting platforms and should not be interpreted as a 
comprehensive measure of gambling activity, betting volume, customer profitability, or platform market share. 
Generations, if discussed, are defined as follows: Gen Z, born after 1995; Younger Millennials: born between 1989-1995; Older Millennials: born 
between 1978-1988; Gen Xers: born between 1965-1977; Baby Boomer: 1946-1964; Traditionalists: pre-1946. 
A Bank of America Market Insights Survey was conducted between March 24-31, 2026, among 2,351 respondents. 
Unless otherwise stated, data is not adjusted for seasonality, processing days or portfolio changes, and may be subject to periodic revisions.  
Additional information about the methodology used to aggregate the data is available upon request.

## Document 293: The revenge of the supply-side_ A new economic regime (2026-09-14)

Source PDF: all report pdf/The revenge of the supply-side_ A new economic regime (2026-09-14).pdf
Report source: Unknown
Date: 未知日期

Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Deutsche Bank 
Research Institute

The revenge of the supply-side:  
A new economic regime

1 
Authors 
Henry Allen 
Strategist

September 14, 2026 
Back in the 2010s, macroeconomic policy was preoccupied by insufficient 
demand. In historical terms, it was an unusual period of low inflation and low 
interest rates, where supply-side issues were considered peripheral. If anything, 
it went almost unquestioned that the supply side of the economy would scale up 
to meet higher demand. 
But in the 2020s so far, we’re back in a supply-driven world. Growth has been 
constrained as much by supply shortages as deficient demand. This year alone, 
we’ve seen major consequences from the closure of the Strait of Hormuz, with 
ramifications for oil prices, inflation and economic growth. 
This macro regime shift, where supply rather than demand is the bigger issue, is 
a tricky environment to navigate, and renders much of the 2010s playbook 
redundant: 
— First, inflation spikes will occur more frequently in a supply-constrained 
world, as there isn’t the spare economic capacity to meet higher 
demand. 
— Second, monetary stimulus also becomes less effective in this 
environment, as traditional demand-side monetary tools can’t deal with 
the direct cause of supply shocks. 
— Third, fiscal stimulus in a supply-constrained environment becomes 
much more inflationary and is more likely to crowd out private 
investment. 
Looking forward, the return to a supply-driven world means that the primary 
issue for policymakers will be expanding the productive frontier of the economy. 
After years where insufficient demand was the main preoccupation, the future of 
growth now rests on a supply-side expansion that can raise living standards 
across the board.

### Page 2

14 September 2026
Thematic Research
Page 2 Deutsche Bank AG
The paradigm shift in thinking: How the biggest economic
problem moved from demand to supply
The legacy of the Global Financial Crisis led to a world of demand deficiency
In 2008, the Global Financial Crisis (GFC) marked the worst economic contraction
since the Great Depression of the 1930s. Unemployment rose to multi-decade
highs and the subsequent recovery was very sluggish, despite huge economic

stimulus on a scale previously unheard of in peacetime. This slow recovery was
clear in numerous ways, but an obvious one was that the US unemployment rate
didn’t return to its pre-GFC low until 2017, a full decade later. Meanwhile in the
Eurozone, it didn’t happen until February 2020, just as the pandemic then sent it
higher once again.
This was a world where deficient demand was the main preoccupation of
macroeconomic policymaking. Indeed, the Depression-era concept of “secular

stagnation” made a comeback, in which the equilibrium interest rate consistent
with full employment was negative. In this world, the constraints of the zero lower
bound on monetary policy would make it very difficult to get to full employment.
This demand deficiency manifested itself in persistent inflation undershoots.
Throughout the 2010s, both the Federal Reserve and the ECB missed their 2%
inflation targets on the downside. Policy rates remained close to the zero lower

bound, and long-term market interest rates also moved continuously lower. Even
before the pandemic, Germany’s 10-year bund yield fell to -0.7% in summer 2019.
In this environment of negative yields, borrowing was basically a “free lunch”.
At the time, there were genuine fears about a permanent low-growth trap. Indeed,
when the Covid-19 pandemic began and another economic contraction was
underway, there were initially concerns that deflationary pressures would intensify

further. For instance, the 10-year US inflation swap (a market-based measure of
expectations for US CPI over the next decade) fell to just 0.81% in March 2020.
Investors genuinely thought that low inflation and low interest rates were likely to
persist for the decade ahead as well, and the 10-year Treasury yield closed at a
record low of 0.51% in August 2020.
This fear of deficient demand informed the policy response to Covid-19. The
stimulus packages went well beyond those seen in the GFC. For instance, no time

was lost in slashing rates back to the zero lower bound, and the fiscal deficits went
far above anything previously seen in peacetime. In fact, the US federal deficit stood
at -14.5% of GDP in 2020, the biggest since 1945. And with much of the global
economy locked down for months, consumers accumulated huge “excess
savings” as they couldn’t spend on their normal activities.
The pandemic was the turning point towards a world of supply constraints

With hindsight, we can see that all this stimulus and the accumulation of excess
savings created a huge demand surge, just as the global economy was
experiencing major supply-chain issues as it reopened:
n The lockdown periods saw a big rotation in spending away from services
(like dining out, travel, gym memberships) towards durable goods (like
home office equipment, electronics, exercise equipment). This
overwhelmed existing supply chains.
n Pandemic-related disruption led to major issues with shipping. A severe

### Page 3

14 September 2026
Thematic Research
Deutsche Bank AG Page 3
shortage of containers led freight rates to rise more than 5-fold at their peak.
n Labour markets were disrupted, as many took early retirement during the
pandemic or changed occupations. Companies therefore had to raise
wages to compete for a smaller pool of workers.
n Commodity prices also surged in this period, particularly energy. The
pandemic period saw producers cut back on drilling and exploration. But

this meant it was more difficult to dial up supply when demand started to
rise again, and energy prices surged back, with Brent crude oil moving
above its pre-pandemic levels in mid-to-late 2021.
These supply constraints marked a major shift from the environment of the 2010s.
Back then, it was almost an unquestioned assumption that the supply-side of the
economy would scale up to meet demand. Inflation had been low and the most
obvious signs of supply-side stress were not apparent. But now the reverse was

true, and inflation was gathering pace again.
Initially, many argued that this burst of inflation would prove temporary. They
argued it was down to one-off factors like the pandemic and the subsequent
reopening. Moreover, there was a view that the longer-term disinflationary forces
of the previous 30 years would still reassert themselves. But by late-2021, it was
clear that the combination of huge stimulus, the drawdown of excess savings, and
serious supply-side disruption had created more inflation than anticipated. By

December 2021, US CPI had risen to its highest level since 1982, at 7.0%, whilst
Eurozone CPI was at its highest since 1991, at 5.0%.

The transition to a supply-constrained world accelerated dramatically in early 2022
with Russia’s invasion of Ukraine. Energy prices rose significantly, and European
industry was forced to decouple from Russian gas. Meanwhile, the blockade of
Ukraine’s Black Sea ports restricted the supply of agricultural commodities like

wheat and corn, driving food prices higher still.
This sent inflation even higher, up to multi-decade highs around the world. US CPI
inflation peaked at 9.1% in June 2022, and Eurozone CPI peaked at 10.6% in
October 2022. This was the context in which central banks launched into the most
aggressive rate hikes in a generation. Over 2022-23, the Federal Reserve hiked rates
by 525bps, and the ECB delivered 450bps.

### Page 4

14 September 2026
Thematic Research
Page 4 Deutsche Bank AG
These supply issues persisted long after the pandemic-related issues were over
Yet even as the worst of that inflation wave passed, the 2023-24 period kept
demonstrating how supply-side disruptions could lead to wider economic
problems. For instance, attacks from the Houthi rebels in the Red Sea forced major
shipping lines to reroute away from the Suez Canal around the Cape of Good Hope,

adding over a week to the length of trips. Meanwhile, an El Nino-induced drought
meant transit capacity through the Panama Canal fell 30%, forcing ships to wait in
long queues or reroute instead. And in Europe, repeated summer heatwaves have
lowered the Rhine’s water levels, disrupting industrial barges and affecting
industries like chemicals and steel.

More broadly, the other shift in the post-pandemic period was the growing use of
strategic industrial policy, particularly as governments sought to boost domestic
manufacturing capacity. For instance, the US passed the Inflation Reduction Act

under the Biden administration, which encouraged clean energy investments, as
well as the CHIPS Act, which sought to boost semiconductor manufacturing. Tariffs
also became a more enduring feature of the landscape, and the Biden
administration hiked tariffs on Chinese imports, with those on EVs up from 25% to
100%, whilst semiconductors rose from 25% to 50%.
Then under the second Trump administration, tariffs became an even bigger part of
the policy toolkit, with the US effective tariff rate surging from 2.6% beforehand to

a peak of 23% in April 2025, before settling around 11% currently. Several different
measures were introduced, but the macro impacts were clear. In particular, there
was a major wave of front-loading of imports in Q1 2025, as those trading
internationally sought to get ahead of the announcements. Indeed, the surge in
imports meant US GDP contracted slightly in Q1 2025. Moreover, other countries
moved to respond, and not just with tariffs of their own. For instance, China

introduced export controls on rare earths.
Whilst 2025 was dominated by tariffs, 2026 then brought a fresh supply shock for
the global economy with the closure of the Strait of Hormuz. Previously, around
25% of the world’s seaborne oil trade had gone through the Strait, including nearly
15 million barrels per day of crude oil. But the start of the US-Iran conflict led the
Strait of Hormuz to close, and six months later only limited traffic is still able to pass

through. This led to another supply shock, with Brent crude oil prices moving back

### Page 5

14 September 2026
Thematic Research
Deutsche Bank AG Page 5
above $100/bbl, whilst European natural gas futures also reached their highest
levels since late-2022. Yet again, the biggest global economic disruption of the year
to date has been a supply shock, rather than a demand one.
Figure 5: US Effective Tariff Rate (%) with projected
postsubstitution rate from the Yale Budget Lab

Types of supply shocks
Broadly speaking, we can distinguish between three sorts of supply shocks,
although there can be considerable overlap between these categories.
1. Temporary supply shocks that have a short-term economic impact.
Example: low river levels blocking a key trade route.

The first category of supply shocks are those that have a temporary impact on the
economy. Examples include the blockage of the Suez Canal in 2021 when the Ever
Given container ship ran aground, or a drought that causes river levels to fall and
means fewer ships can get through key waterways.
These are supply shocks that temporarily remove capacity, but then see a bounce
back to “normal”, broadly speaking. For instance, a drought is hopefully not

repeated, and when river levels return to normal, supply can resume again.
For the economy, this means the longer-term consequences are also limited. The
inflation impact is mean-reverting, and output recovers, often in a V-shape. These
are the sort of shocks that central banks are usually able to “look through”, because
they don’t cause permanent structural damage to the economy.
2.Medium-term supply shocks that can last several years, with a clear economic

impact.
Example: the closure of the Strait of Hormuz.
These kinds of shocks are often driven by geopolitical events or policy decisions,
and are longer-lasting shocks which do not have a quick solution. A good example
is the current closure of the Strait of Hormuz, but we have seen many in recent years.
In theory, these shocks can end, but their length is uncertain and they have material
economic impacts. An increase in tariffs also fits into this category, as it forces an

adjustment in trade flows, even if they can be reversed in theory.

### Page 6

14 September 2026
Thematic Research
Page 6 Deutsche Bank AG
A good example of this kind of supply shock is the 1973 oil shock. Back in 1973, the
oil embargo by OAPEC led oil prices to nearly quadruple, and they remained at that
higher level for years after. It wasn’t simply a temporary adjustment that normalised
again.
Unlike the short-term shocks, these medium-term shocks can force a permanent
rerouting of trade flows and supply chains. Moreover, they can push inflation higher

for an extended period and are more likely to filter through into core inflation
categories. And because they are not temporary shocks that quickly adjust,
policymakers can’t “look through” them as easily. As such, central banks aren’t
able to accommodate the shock, and often have to hike rates to clamp down on the
resulting inflation, as we saw in 2022.
3.Structural supply trends that affect the longer-term growth rate.
Example: Demographic trends.

This category includes the long-term forces on the supply side that affect the
economy’s growth rate. A good example are demographics and the current
situation of ageing populations. Another example are the multi-decade processes
of globalization and deglobalization.
These are factors that can permanently shift the productive frontier of the economy.
For example, the current situation of ageing populations means that a smaller
proportion of the population are of working-age, meaning that labour forces are

shrinking. As a result, that constrains the growth rate of potential GDP, meaning
that more growth must come from higher productivity rather than a larger number
of workers.
These shifting trends can lead to permanent changes in so-called r*, which is the
equilibrium interest rate where full employment is achieved – a level that neither
stimulates nor restricts economic activity. In other words, these factors are capable
of shifting the economy’s potential growth rate, at which it can grow sustainably

without generating inflationary pressures.
What happened the last time the supply-side dominated:
Lessons from the 1970s
As we consider the return of the supply side, it’s worth bearing in mind this is far
from the first time that supply shocks have dominated the landscape. Back in the
1970s, the biggest economic shocks of that decade were also supply driven.
That decade saw two huge oil shocks in 1973 and 1979. In the first, the Organization
of Arab Petroleum Exporting Countries (OAPEC) placed an embargo on several

countries, including the United States, in retaliation for their support for Israel in the
Yom Kippur War. This caused oil prices to nearly quadruple, triggering a recession
across multiple countries that began in late 1973. Then in 1979, a second oil shock
occurred around the time of the Iranian Revolution. That coincided with a big
decline in Iran’s oil output, before the Iran-Iraq War in 1980 led to further production
declines for both countries. As a result, oil prices more than doubled again.

To be fair, there were other demand-side and supply-side shocks that meant
inflation got out of hand in the 1970s. But the two oil shocks were the single biggest
factors, and created a stagflationary environment in which inflation accelerated

### Page 7

14 September 2026
Thematic Research
Deutsche Bank AG Page 7
and growth slowed.
Figure 7: WTI Oil Prices ($/bbl) in real terms
0

crisis

Source : Finaeon, Deutsche Bank
This experience is instructive for today, as the 1970s demonstrated how a repeated
set of supply shocks could act in a cumulative way. After all, even if the textbook

response is to “look through” a temporary supply shock, a series of consecutive
shocks can suddenly feel very permanent when they happen one-after-another.
That continuous disruption can then cause inflation expectations to become
unanchored, risking the kind of dynamics that trigger wage-price spirals. Indeed,
much as economists can focus on “core inflation” that excludes food and energy
prices, these are essential goods that are difficult to substitute away from, and play

a key role in inflation expectations.
On the bright side, the more optimistic lesson is that supply shocks often force
adjustments that prevent a repeat. For instance, back in the 1970s, there was a
recognition that Western economies had become overly dependent on Middle
Eastern oil, and major efforts were made to reduce that dependence. For instance,
the US established the Strategic Petroleum Reserve ( SPR) in 1975, and the
Department of Energy was established in 1977. Moreover, investments were

directed to US energy production, and since 2019, the US has been a net exporter
of energy, rather than a net importer.
Clearly the 1970s are far from an exact parallel. Most obviously, economies are far
less energy intensive today, so oil shocks don’t have as big an impact today.
Nevertheless, the dynamic of repeated supply shocks is one that bears relevance
given how that dynamic is re-echoing today, and with inflation still lingering above
target in many of the world’s biggest economies.

What are the implications for today?
1. Inflation spikes will occur more frequently, as there isn’t the spare economic

### Page 8

14 September 2026
Thematic Research
Page 8 Deutsche Bank AG
capacity to meet higher demand.
The first consequence of a supply-constrained world is that unlike much of the
2010s, there isn’t the spare economic capacity (or a negative output gap) that can
easily absorb higher demand. So if there is an unexpected demand shock, for
whatever reason, the consequence will likely be higher inflation rather than higher
output.
Figure 8: IMF: Output Gap in the G7 economies (% of potential GDP)

-5
-4
-3
-2
-1
0
1
2
3
1980 1990 2000 2010 2020
The 2010s environment of
deficient demand has gone
Source : Haver Analytics, Deutsche Bank
This is a particular risk right now because of those longer-term structural shifts on
the supply side. First, global demographics and the retirement of the baby boomers
mean that populations are ageing, and many countries now even have shrinking
working-age populations. This is a big difference from the world of decades past,

when the labour force was generally expanding. Second, the push to reshore
production of key industries has also raised costs, as it requires the duplication of
supply chains. And third, other structural shifts like the push for higher defence
spending have also created an increase in the demand for capital. So there isn't the
spare capacity in the economy to absorb higher demand that there was in the
2010s.
This world of more frequent supply shocks means that central banks must also

trade off how quickly they want to bring inflation back to target against the potential
output volatility that might result. Indeed, we saw this year when the Strait of
Hormuz was blocked, central banks didn’t immediately react with rate hikes, but
they waited a few months to see how the consequences developed. But of course,
that has also meant inflation has stayed above target in many countries, often for
several years. In fact, US PCE inflation has been above 2% since March 2021.

2. Monetary stimulus becomes less effective, as it can’t deal with the direct cause
of supply shocks.
Monetary policy has a complex transmission channel, but when central banks cut
rates, the aim is usually to stimulate demand. This is a very useful tool in demand-
driven recessions like 2008, but it becomes more problematic in the face of supply
shocks.
The problem is that when a supply shock hits, whether that’s a natural disaster, or
the blockage of a key trade route, the economy’s productive capacity drops.

### Page 9

14 September 2026
Thematic Research
Deutsche Bank AG Page 9
Monetary policy cannot affect that directly.
This matters because negative supply shocks have the unfortunate effect of raising
inflation and lowering growth simultaneously. So it puts central banks in a very
tricky position, as they found in the inflationary environment of the 1970s after the
oil shocks. With hindsight, many central banks were too dovish after the first oil
shock and inflation got out of control. In the case of the Federal Reserve, that then

led to a far more hawkish response to the second oil shock, when Paul Volcker
tightened monetary policy significantly to get inflation under control again.
These problems are further exacerbated because central banks operate with
uncertainty about the future. Just as we don’t know today when the Strait of
Hormuz might return to normal, central bankers in 1973 were unsure how long the
oil embargo would last. But because central banks make policy that responds with

a lag, there is inherently the risk of overshooting. If they expect a supply shock to be
permanent, yet it proves temporary, then they risk overtightening. And similarly in
reverse, if disruption is expected to be temporary, but proves permanent, they risk
not doing enough and letting inflation and inflation expectations rise too much.
3. Fiscal stimulus becomes much more likely to cause overheating and crowding
out.
In a supply-constrained world where the economy lacks spare capacity, fiscal

stimulus is more likely to overheat the economy and generate inflation.
This is a big shift from the post-GFC world. Back then, unemployment was high and
deficient demand meant additional government spending didn’t face the same
competition from private investment, so the multipliers from that stimulus were
much higher. Moreover, several countries were able to borrow at incredibly low
interest rates, even negative in some cases.
But this shifted with the pandemic. Not only was the stimulus in 2020 far bigger, but

unlike 2008, the economy then saw a huge wave of pent-up demand once the
pandemic restrictions were lifted, supported by the excess savings accumulated
during the lockdowns. In turn, that contributed to a huge wave of inflation, even
before we then had the additional energy and food price shocks in 2022.
Meanwhile, government bond yields have also returned to their pre-GFC levels,
making the cost of any stimulus far more expensive. And that comes as we are also

seeing a kind of "reverse crowding out", thanks to borrowing from the AI
hyperscalers that is adding to pressure on rates. So in this environment, any fiscal
stimulus will come at a higher cost.
Conclusion: Looking forward
The transition to a supply-driven world means that many of the old economic
assumptions from the 2010s are no longer valid. There’s been a structural break
that means classic demand-side stimulus is not as effective. After years when it was

almost unquestioned that the supply side of the economy would scale up to meet
higher demand, we are now back in a world where supply is a meaningful constraint
again.
As such, the emphasis will naturally shift towards expanding the supply-side of the

### Page 10

14 September 2026
Thematic Research
Page 10 Deutsche Bank AG
economy, something we’re already beginning to see. This could be encouraging
more energy production, more housing, or bringing new participants into the
labour force. In essence, what’s needed are policies that raise the productive
potential of the economy, and boost the rate at which the economy can grow
without generating inflationary pressures.
These supply shocks in recent years have been a challenge. Even if they might be

temporary in isolation, the cumulative effect has been to keep inflation elevated,
and we saw that happen back in the 1970s too. This is particularly problematic if the
starting point is above-target inflation, as further supply shocks risk unanchoring
inflation expectations.
Nevertheless, one consequence of a world of more frequent supply shocks is that
actions are then taken to reduce vulnerabilities over the years ahead. For instance,
the 1970s oil shocks led to the widespread realisation that the US needed to

become less energy intensive and produce more of its own energy. As a result, the
Strategic Petroleum Reserve was set up, and the subsequent years saw the US
eventually become a net energy exporter. Similarly today, the closure of the Strait
of Hormuz has forced other countries to secure alternative supply routes and use
strategic reserves. So even before the longer-term geopolitical outcome is clear, we
have seen diversification away from one particular chokepoint. In a supply-driven

world, the incentives will shift towards making those supply chains more resilient,
and better able to withstand future shocks.

## Document 316: “木头姐”预测SpaceX星舰发射将创造10万亿美元收入，马斯克回应：并非不可能

Source PDF: all report pdf/“木头姐”预测SpaceX星舰发射将创造10万亿美元收入，马斯克回应：并非不可能.pdf
Report source: Unknown
Date: 未知日期
Pages: 4
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

“ ⽊头姐 ”Cathie Wood 预测，星舰单次发射或对应约 10 亿美元年收⼊，若按⻢斯克 2030 年
年发射 1 万次的⽬标推算，年收⼊规模可能达 10 万亿美元，⻢斯克回应 “ 并⾮不可能 ” 。这⼀
测算基于 Starlink V3 的带宽变现潜⼒，星舰第 14 次⻜⾏将搭载量产版 V3 卫星，并成为⾸次
产⽣收⼊的⻜⾏。
摘要：
SpaceX 星舰的商业化想象空间正在被进⼀步放⼤。 ARK Invest ⾸席执⾏官 Cathie Wood （ “ ⽊头
姐 ” ）最新预测称，星舰每次发射或可带来约 10 亿美元年度收⼊，若按照⻢斯克提出的年发射⽬
标推算，到 2030 年星舰年收⼊规模甚⾄可能达到 10 万亿美元。
这⼀预测迅速引发市场关注。⻢斯克本⼈在社交平台 X 上回应 Wood 的相关帖⽂，仅⽤三个英⽂
单词表⽰ “It's not impossible” （并⾮不可能），为这⼀极具争议的收⼊预测增添了讨论热度。
Wood 的判断，很⼤程度上建⽴在⻢斯克对星舰发射频率的激进⽬标之上。此前，⻢斯克在 X 平

台表⽰，SpaceX 的⽬标是到 2030 年实现每天 30 次以上的星舰发射，年化发射次数达到约 1 万
次，并称与⻜机航班相⽐，这仍然是⼀个很⼩的数字。
Wood 进⼀步将这⼀发射能⼒与 Starlink 的商业化潜⼒联系起来。 ARK Invest 数据显⽰， Starlink
近年来在轨累计带宽持续增⻓，⽽ SpaceX 下⼀代 V3 卫星拥有更⾼的单星带宽，叠加星舰远超现
⾸⻚ 资讯 快讯 ⾏情 ⽇历 APP VIP 会员 ⼤师课 ⽣活家 搜索

### Page 2

有⽕箭的运⼒，有望⼤幅提升单次发射所能部署的⽹络容量，从⽽扩⼤ Starlink 的服务能⼒和收
⼊空间。
与此同时， SpaceX 即将开始部署 V3 卫星，星舰的商业化能⼒也将迎来实际验证。公司⾸席财务
官 Bret Johnsen 表⽰，星舰第 14 次⻜⾏将搭载量产版 V3 卫星，并将成为星舰 “ ⾸次产⽣收⼊的⻜
⾏ ”。这意味着，星舰能否从⾼频测试逐步⾛向可持续的商业发射，将开始有实际收⼊数据作为
验证。
从 Starlink 带宽到单次发射 10 亿美元收⼊
Wood 的预测源于 ARK Invest 战略与投资总监 Sam Korus 的分析框架。 Korus 根据 2023 年⾄ 2025
年的数据测算， Starlink 在轨累计带宽分别达到 190 、 335 和 606 Tbps ，对应通信收⼊分别为 39
亿、 76 亿和 114 亿美元。
由此计算，每 Tbps 带宽对应的年度收⼊分别约为 2100 万、 2300 万和 1900 万美元。Korus 以最新
的 1900 万美元作为单位带宽收⼊，再乘以星舰单次发射可部署的约 61 Tbps 带宽容量，得出 “ 每

次星舰发射约带来 10 亿美元年度收⼊ ” 的结论。
这⼀测算的核⼼在于 V3 卫星的容量。 SpaceX 披露，每颗 V3 卫星的下⾏带宽设计为 1 Tbps ，星
舰单次最多可部署约 60 颗 V3 卫星，对应超过 60 Tbps 的新增带宽，整体带宽吞吐量超过猎鹰 9 号
发射任务的 20 倍。
Korus 同时指出，随着 Starlink 带宽供给不断扩⼤，单位 Tbps 收⼊可能逐步下降，这意味着上述
10 亿美元并不是固定不变的收⼊⽔平。不过，即便单位带宽收⼊下降，更⼤的卫星部署规模仍
可能推动 Starlink 通信收⼊持续增⻓。
星舰⾼频发射，能否⽀撑 10 万亿美元收⼊？
在 Korus 测算基础上， Wood 进⼀步将单次发射的收⼊潜⼒与⻢斯克的⻓期发射⽬标联系起来。
她在 X 平台表⽰，⻢斯克计划到 2030 年实现每年 1 万次星舰发射，据此推算，星舰年度收⼊规模
可能达到 10 万亿美元。
这⼀数字与⻢斯克此前提出的收⼊⽬标存在明显差距。今年 6 ⽉，⻢斯克曾表⽰， SpaceX“ 或

许 ” 能在 2030 年实现约 1 万亿美元收⼊；⽽公司 2025 年收⼊为 186.7 亿美元。因此， 10 万亿美元
⽬前仍是基于发射频次和带宽变现能⼒的外部推演，并⾮ SpaceX 官⽅指引。
不过，这⼀收⼊逻辑正进⼊现实验证阶段。⻢斯克本周⼆确认， SpaceX 将于本⽉开始部署下⼀
代 Starlink V3 星座，并称其最终带宽将超过现有约 1.1 万颗卫星⽹络的 100 倍。 SpaceX ⽬前计划
于 9 ⽉ 22 ⽇执⾏星舰第 14 次⻜⾏任务，待监管审批后启动。
要实现每年 1 万次发射，星舰⾃⾝的制造能⼒也是关键约束。⻢斯克近⽇谈及猛禽 3 （ Raptor 3 ）
时表⽰，会建议初创公司不要尝试制造这款发动机，因为其内部结构极为复杂，只有经过⾼度
改装的⾦属 3D 打印技术才能制造。
更重要的是，第 14 次⻜⾏将搭载量产版 V3 卫星。 SpaceX ⾸席财务官 Bret Johnsen 此前表⽰，这
将成为星舰 “ ⾸次产⽣收⼊的⻜⾏ ” 。随着 V3 开始部署，星舰的发射频次、 Starlink 容量增量以及

单次发射的收⼊转化能⼒，也将从纸⾯测算逐步进⼊实际验证。

### Page 3

⻛险提⽰及免责条款

中的任何意⻅、观点或结论是否符合其特定状况。据此投资，责任⾃负。

華邦電 2344 TT  ( 電子–半導體 ) 
華開盛景，節節登巔 
投資與評價 
1. 華邦電 2Q26 受惠記憶體供需緊俏及 AI 與網通需求強勁 ，CMS 混合 ASP
季增約 100%，EPS 5.40 元， 營運創新高 ；3Q26 受惠 CMS 合約價續漲，
以及 NOR Flash 與 SLC NAND 供給收斂 ，EPS 7.67 元 ， 營運續創新高。 
2. 華邦電 26 年受惠 DRAM 與 Flash 價格上行、20nm CMS 與先進 SLC 
NAND 比重提升，產能維持近滿載，EPS 25.84 元；27 年高雄廠 16nm
產能放量，Enterprise SSD、CUBE 與 Si-Cap 貢獻提升，EPS 41.23。 
3. 目前華邦電股價交易在 180.0 元，約為 1.9X 2027E PB，位於近五年參
考 PBR 區間 0.7~4.0x 中間偏下位置，考量27 年在供需缺口延續、價格
維持高檔下，營運可望續創新高，維持買進投資建議，目標價由 158 元
(2.4 x 修正前 27(F) PB)調升至 230 元(2.4 x 修正後 27(F) PB)。 
重點更新 
華邦電 2Q26 DRAM 與 Flash 漲價帶動營運大幅成長 
全球 DRAM 供給維持緊俏，主要供應商持續縮減 DDR4 及成熟製程位元供
給，而 AI 伺服器、資料中心交換器、Router、Gateway 及網路基礎設施需
求推升記憶體用量 ， 帶動營收598.43 億元(+56.4% QoQ)。在CMS 與 Flash
價格同 步上 漲、20nm 及先進 SLC NAND 占比 提升， 毛利率66.2% 
(+12.8ppts QoQ)。隨營運規模快速擴大，並持續投入 16nm DRAM、先進
SLC NAND 、CUBE 及 Si-Cap 等產品研發，營業費用 106.66 億元(+35.6% 
QoQ)，營業利益 289.79 億元(+130.9% QoQ) ，稅後淨利 241.99 億元
(+139.2% QoQ)，EPS 5.40 元，優於預期且創歷史新高。 
預估華邦電 3Q26 記憶體價格續漲與產能滿載推升營運再創新高 
網路基礎設施及 Edge AI 需求則持續增加單機記憶體容量 ， 使DRAM 供需維
持緊俏，帶動 3Q26 CMS 合約價格延續上行。因調高 ASP 預估，故上修營
收至 837.96 億元(+40.0% QoQ)。在 CMS、NOR Flash 及 SLC NAND 價格
持續上漲、20nm CMS 與 F24 產品占比提高下，上調毛利率預估4.5 個百分
點至 68.7%(+2.5ppts QoQ) 。在 16nm DRAM 、F24 與後續 19nm SLC 
NAND、CUBE 及 Si-Cap 研發持續推進下，調升營業費用至 143.57 億元
(+34.6% QoQ)，調高營業利益至 432.31 億元(+49.2% QoQ)，上修稅後淨
利至 343.23 億元(+41.8% QoQ)，EPS 7.67 元，營運續創歷史新高。

### Page 2

報告內容 
華邦電 2Q26 DRAM 與 Flash 漲價帶動營運大幅成長 
全球 DRAM 供給維持緊俏，主要供應商持續縮減 DDR4 及成熟製程位元供給，而 AI 伺服器、
資料中心交換器、Router、Gateway 及網路基礎設施需求推升記憶體用量，帶動 2Q26 記憶體
事業營收 517.00 億元(+72.8% QoQ)，其中 CMS 雖因位元出貨量季減低十位數百分比，但受
惠價格上漲及產品組合改善，混合平均售價季增約 100%，且 20nm 產品占 CMS 營收比重提
升至 71%；Flash 則受高容量 NOR Flash 、Enterprise SSD 及 SLC NAND 需求帶動，位元出
貨量季增中十位數百分比、混合平均售價季增低四十位數百分比，24nm 及 32nm SLC NAND
占 Flash 營收比重合計提升至約 41%，帶動營收 598.43 億元(+56.4% QoQ) ，優於預期。在
CMS 與 Flash 價格同步上漲、20nm 及先進 SLC NAND 占比提升，以及記憶體產能利用率維
持接近 100%帶動固定成本吸收改善下，毛利率 由前一季之 53.4%提升至 66.2%(+12.8ppts 
QoQ)。隨營運規模快速擴大，並持續投入 16nm DRAM、先進 SLC NAND、CUBE 及 Si-Cap
等產品研發，營業費用 106.66 億元(+35.6% QoQ)，營業利益 289.79 億元(+130.9% QoQ)，
稅後淨利 241.99 億元(+139.2% QoQ)，EPS 5.40 元，優於預期且創歷史新高。

預估華邦電 3Q26 記憶體價格續漲與產能滿載推升營運再創新高 
AI 伺服器、資料中心、網路基礎設施及 Edge AI 需求則持續增加單機記憶體容量，使 DRAM
供需維持緊俏，帶動 3Q26 CMS 合約價格延續上行。Flash 方面，高容量 NOR Flash 持續受
AI 伺服器需求支撐，新世代 AI 伺服器機架可能使用約 500 至 600 顆 NOR Flash，雖然供不應
求，但NOR Flash 定價仍將兼顧市占率及客戶關係，不主動帶頭大幅漲價。SLC NAND 則受主
要供應商逐步淡出 2D NAND 與低容量 eMMC 帶動，部分 4GB 及 8GB eMMC 需求轉向 SLC 
NAND，供需持續吃緊 。因調高 ASP 預估 ， 故上修營收預估 38.4%至 837.96 億元(+40.0% QoQ)。
在 CMS、NOR Flash 及 SLC NAND 價格持續上漲、20nm CMS 與 F24 產品占比提高及產能
利用率維持近滿載下，上調毛利率預估 4.5 個百分點至 68.7%(+2.5ppts QoQ)。

### Page 3

在 16nm DRAM、F24 與後續 19nm SLC NAND 、CUBE 及 Si-Cap 研發持續推進下，調升營
業費用至 143.57 億元(+34.6% QoQ)，調高營業利益預估 59.5%至 432.31 億元(+49.2% QoQ)，
上修稅後淨利預估 60.7%至 343.23 億元(+41.8% QoQ)，EPS 7.67 元，營運續創歷史新高。

預估華邦電 26 年擴產與價格上行推升營運續創新高 
全球 DRAM 供應商持續將資源轉向 HBM 及先進 DRAM，使DDR4 及成熟製程位元供給收縮，
而 AI 伺服器、資料中心、網通、車用、工業及 Edge AI 應用持續提升記憶體需求，帶動 CMS
價格全年維持高檔；Flash 方面，華邦電NOR Flash 全球市占率約 23%居第一、SLC NAND 市
占率約 15%居第三 ， 高容量NOR Flash 受 AI 伺服器需求增加帶動 ， 新世代AI 伺服器機架可能
使用約 500 至 600 顆 NOR Flash ，預估可取得約 40%至 50%的供應份額，同時主要 NAND
供應商逐步退出 2D NAND 及低容量 eMMC 市場 ， 帶動SLC NAND 需求轉移 ， 加上低軌衛星、
FWA、AI Camera、Edge AI Gateway、機器人、無人機、車用通訊、穿戴及 AR／VR 等新興
應用擴張，使供需持續吃緊。 
台中廠 NOR 與 NAND 產能持續擴充，高雄廠 Module A 月產能由 15K 向年底 24K 提升，搭
配 20nm CMS、F24 SLC NAND 及 MCP 等產品組合優化，因上調 ASP 預估，上修 26 年營收
預估 37.6%至 2,912.00 億元(+225.7% YoY) 。在 DRAM 與 Flash 價格大幅上漲、產品組合往
20nm 及先進 SLC NAND 移動，以及產能利用率維持接近滿載帶動固定成本吸收改善下，調升
毛利率預估 3.5 個百分點至 66.7%(+31.8ppts YoY)。 隨營運規模大幅擴張及員工相關費用增加 ，
並持續投入 16nm DRAM、F24 與 19nm SLC NAND、CUBE、Si-Cap 及 MCP 等產品研發，
調高營業費用 預估 18.5%至 501.61 億元(+95.5% YoY) ，上修營業利益 至 1,440.66 億元
(+2,503.3% YoY)。上調稅後淨利預估 61.3%至 1,158.22 億元(+3,545.6% YoY)，EPS 25.84 元，
營運創歷史新高。

### Page 4

預估華邦電 27 年受惠供需缺口延續與 16nm 放量驅動營運創高峰 
全球 DRAM 供給預期較 26 年進一步吃緊，因新增產能建置時間較長，短期供給成長仍主要依
靠製程微縮及良率改善，而 AI 伺服器、傳統伺服器、Inference、Edge AI 與 On-Device AI 需
求持續增加 ， 華邦電高雄廠Module A 月產能於 26 年底提升至 24K 後，16nm 產能逐步放量，
良率亦由目前接近 80%朝 90%提升，帶動27 年高雄廠位元產出可望接近倍增，台中廠DRAM
月產能則由約 12K 逐步降至 7K 並配合製程與產品組合轉換。 
Flash 方面，F24 與後續 19nm 製程持續推進，Enterprise SSD Cache 營收可望由目前接近零
提升至記憶體事業營收約 10%，CUBE 憑藉高頻寬及低功耗特性切入 Inference 與 Edge Server，
預估占記憶體事業營收約 5%，Si-Cap 則受 AI 與先進封裝電源管理需求推動，既有獨立產能已
售罄且不排擠 16nm DRAM 產能 ，上修 27 年營收預估 34.3%至 4,704.87 億元(+61.6% YoY)。
雖高雄及台中擴產設備折舊陸續增加形成部分成本壓力，但在 16nm 良率提升、先進製程占比
增加 、 產品組合改善及產能維持高檔下 ，上修毛利率預估 5.3 個百分點至 67.2%(+0.5ppts YoY)。
隨 16nm 與 19nm 製程持續優化，並加大 CUBE、Si-Cap 及後續 14nm、12nm 與 EUV 相關
製程技術開發， 調升營業費用 預估 25.1%至 836.82 億元(+66.8% YoY) ，調高營業利益 至
2,323.43 億元(+61.3% YoY)，上修稅後淨利預估 57.7%至 1,848.44 億元(+59.6% YoY)，EPS 
41.23 元，營運再創歷史新高。

記憶體供需缺口未見改善，華邦電擴產驅動長期成長 
華邦電營運已進入高速成長階段，受惠全球 DRAM 與 Flash 供給持續收斂，AI 伺服器、資料
中心、網通及 Edge AI 等需求強勁，帶動 CMS、NOR Flash 與 SLC NAND 價格持續上行，加
上 20nm DRAM 與先進 SLC NAND 產品比重提升、產能利用率維持近滿載，推升營收與獲利
快速成長。26 年在價格上漲、產品組合改善及台中 NOR/NAND 與高雄 DRAM 產能擴充帶動
下，營運可望大幅成長；27 年則隨高雄廠 16nm 產能進一步開出、NAND 位元需求維持高成
長，以及Enterprise SSD DRAM Cache、CUBE 與 Si-Cap 等 AI 相關產品開始放大營收貢獻，
成長動能由價格上漲進一步延伸至位元出貨與新產品放量。目前華邦電股價交易在 180.0 元，
約為 1.9X 2027E PB，位於近五年參考 PBR 區間 0.7~4.0x 中間偏下位置，考量 27 年在供需缺
口延續、價格維持高檔下，營運可望續創新高，維持買進投資建議，目標價由 158 元(2.4 x 修
正前 27(F) PB)調升至 230 元(2.4 x 修正後 27(F) PB)。

### Page 5

公司簡介 
華邦電(2344.TT)成立於 1987 年，為台灣少數採IDM 模式之記憶體廠商，具備自有晶圓廠、製
程開發與產品設計整合能力 ， 總部位於台中中部科學園區 ， 並於台中及高雄設有12 吋晶圓廠。
公司長期聚焦利基型記憶體市場，產品涵蓋 Code Storage Flash(NOR/NAND/安全記憶體)、
客製化記憶體解決方案(CMS，包含 Specialty DRAM 與 Mobile DRAM )以及多晶片整合產品
(MCP)，並透過子公司新唐科技切入邏輯 IC 領域，形成記憶體與控制晶片之平台化布局。相較
於以標準型 DRAM 為主的國際大廠，華邦電採取差異化策略，深耕車用、工業、通訊及 AIoT
等長生命週期應用市場，具備產品多樣化、客戶黏著度高與價格相對穩定等特性。 在產品與應
用面 ， 世界先進長期聚焦在類比 、 混合訊號與電源管理等功能導向晶片所需的製程與製造能力，
這類產品通常比起先進邏輯晶片更重視高壓、高可靠度、良率穩定與一致性，因此常見的終端
市場包含車用電子、工業控制、消費性電子與顯示相關應用。

圖一. Revenue by Application

資料來源: Winbond、國泰證期研究部整理

從營運結構觀察，華邦電記憶體業務以 Flash 與 CMS 為雙核心主軸，其中 NOR Flash 受惠於
低功耗與安全性需求提升，持續拓展至車用電子與AIoT 應用，而CMS 則透過 20nm 至 16nm
製程升級，逐步提升容量密度與成本競爭力，並切入 Edge AI 與高頻寬應用領域。此外，公司
透過高可靠度產品認證與長期供貨能力，建立車用與工業市場進入門檻，形成穩定獲利基礎。
整體而言，華邦電並非典型循環性記憶體廠，而是結合利基市場、製程自主與產品客製能力之
記憶體 IDM，未來成長動能將取決於 CMS 製程升級進度、Flash 產品結構優化，以及車用與
AIoT 滲透率持續提升。

### Page 6

華邦電 ESG 政策 
華邦電主要為 DRAM 和 Flash 之研發、設計、製造與銷售商，設計，產業類別歸類在半導體行
業中，依據專門為投資人設計的永續揭露框架 SASB 準則，半導體行業關注重點為下列幾項：
溫室氣體排放、能源管理、水和廢水管理、廢棄物和危險材料管理、員工健康與安全、員工多
元化和包容性 、 產品設計與生命週期管理 、 材料採購與效率。 由於華邦電有設立工廠從事生產，
研究部將篇幅著重在溫室氣體排放、能源管理、水和廢水管理，並因員工對於半導體產業而言
是相當重要的資產，故員工相關議題也會著重於下描述。 
1. 溫室氣體排放： 
華邦主要溫室氣體排放量為製程使用的  FCs 與外購電力，占範疇一與範疇二溫室氣體排放量  
94% 以上，因此直接排放 FCs 削減(包括製程利用率的提升與加裝尾氣處理設備 )與間接排放
用電節能是主要目標。2022 年單位溫室氣體排放強度指標－「生產 12 吋晶圓每層光罩平均
溫室氣體排放量」為 13.2 公斤二氧化碳當量，與 2021 年 12.6 公斤二氧化碳當量相比，單
位產品碳排放量 YoY 增加約 4.8%(公斤二氧化碳當量 / 層 - 晶圓光罩 )，係因 2022 年全
球經濟衰退等因素導致產能下降，單位產品平均排放量也有所上升；與  2022 年目標值 13.3 
公斤二氧化碳當量相比，YoY 減少約 0.8%。

2. 能源管理： 
近年因廠區擴建，新增機台設備，故各項原物料／燃料使用量呈上升趨勢， 2022 年能源總消
耗量約 2,807,178 十億焦耳。有鑒於此，華邦於  2022 年持續推行節電措施、更換節約能源
設備共 21 件。節電量較 2021 年增加約 129,229 十億焦耳。2022 年單位產品能源消耗量
指標：生產 12 吋晶圓每層光罩，平均能源消耗量為 82 百萬焦耳，與 2021 年 79.3 百萬焦
耳相比，單位產品用電量 YoY 增加約 3.4%(百萬焦耳 / 層 - 晶圓光罩)；與 2022 年目標值 
80.1 百萬焦耳相比，單位產品用電量 YoY 增加約 2.4%(百萬焦耳 / 層 - 晶圓光罩)。此係因 
2022 年全球經濟衰退等因素導致產能需求下降，因而使單位產品平均用電略為超過目標，未來
持續推動節能計畫減輕環境負擔。

3. 水和廢水管理: 
華邦用水來源主要為台灣自來水公司供應之自來水 ， 2022 年華邦總用水量約 4,130,000 立方
公尺。單位產品用水量指標 - 生產 12 吋晶圓每層光罩平均用水量為 134 公升，達到 2022 
年目標(≦ 145 公升／層 - 晶圓光罩)。 
華邦持續提高回收水再利用率，2022 年全廠用水回收率約 80.5%、製程水回收率約 89.3%，
符合科學園區環評承諾(全廠水回收率大於 77%、製程水回收率大於 85%)。華邦 2022 年新
推行 3 件節水措施 ， 節水量增加約 6.4 萬立方公尺 。2018 年至 2022 年累計節水量達 3,530 
百萬公升，約相當於 0.7 座寶山水庫有效容量。

### Page 7

4. 員工健康與安全 
2022 年事故主要肇災項目為物品掉落導致砸傷、化學品噴濺傷害與人員跌倒事故各 1 案；承
攬商部分則無事故 ；2020-2022 年間無職業病死亡或經職業醫學科專科醫師判定確診職業病或
工作相關疾病個案。 
2022 年員工失能傷害件數 3 件，失能傷害嚴重率為 4，失能傷害頻率為 0.43，皆小於行業
平均值，但失能傷害頻率 (FR) 仍未達到設定之目標。2022 年度 3 件事故皆發生於高雄廠，
高雄廠為 2022 年完成之新建廠區，尚在加強內部員工安全意識，其中  2 件為非工作期間所
發生之事故，因此除強化各種作業預防再發手段外，也要求各單位主管也需透過主管巡檢與安
全觀察等活動，將安全意識的重要性傳達給與第一線的員工。

5. 員工多元化和包容性： 
華邦定期檢視員工組成、擬訂優質人力招募策略，據以招募適合的專業人才，維持產競爭力。
2022 年台灣總部碩、博士進用比例達 50%，學士員工則占 35%，以因應半導體業所需知識
密集型的產業特性 。 在年齡分佈上 ， 華邦嚴守國內外勞動法規及責任商業聯盟 (RBA) 行為準則 ，
由人資部門確認應徵者之實際年齡，落實不僱用童工或未完成義務教育年齡者，確保任用年齡
均為 18 歲以上，一般職員年齡介於 31 歲至 50 歲者約占總員工數 73%

東南亞投資攻略

熱門議題 / 投資關鍵
《東協急漲已反映穩健基本面，短線暫時轉中立》
8月以來東協指數上漲 2.5%，升抵中東地緣衝突爆
發以來新高 ，技術指標 RSI亦一度攀升至 83，顯示
市場短線情緒偏熱 。不過，有別於7月普漲行情，8
月漲勢主要由新加坡及基期較低的印尼所帶動 ，區
域內市場表現分歧有所擴大 。展望後市，本行認為，
6月以來的強勁反彈不僅反映第二季穩健的經濟數據，
也已在一定程度上提前反映第三季景氣維持高檔的
預期，推升東協指數本益比來到近兩年平均值加兩
倍標準差附近 。此外，外資連續兩個月淨買超雙印
股市，過往通常多發生於亞洲出口動能放緩期間 。

雖然目前尚難據此判斷資金配置趨勢已出現明確轉
折，但是否反映外資對亞洲出口前景轉趨審慎 ，並
提前布局受外需影響較低的市場 ，仍值得持續觀察 。
市場投資展望
資產 3Q26看法 投資展望
東南亞 中性 短線漲幅及評價已反映基本面，因此下調至中性。雖然多數主要市場估值
仍低於長期平均，但需要新的利多帶動評價回升。
印度 正向 印度持續分散石油供給來源，降低中東影響性。在歷經本輪石油危機後，
印度將加速投資能源自主相關基礎建設，帶動相關類股中長期表現。

要市場表現不均 P.1
• 東協經濟數據在中東地緣衝突爆
發下仍表現穩健 P.2

《東協指數8月創波段新高，但主要市場表現不均》
東協股市自6月低點以來大漲逾14%，惟區域內漲勢
高度集中於新加坡與印尼 ，新加坡更因股價頻創新
高而推升本益比至長期均值之上 。若接下來全球風
險偏好維持穩定，不排除資金持續在東協市場內部進
行輪動，但估值要全面提升可能需要新的利多帶動。
值得留意的是 ，外資近期已連續兩個月加碼印尼與
印度等內需驅動型市場 ，此情況過去多發生於亞洲
出口動能放緩期間 。雖然目前尚難據此判斷資金配
置趨勢已出現明確轉折 ，但是否反映外資對亞洲出
口前景轉趨審慎 ，並提前布局受外需影響較低的市
場，仍值得持續觀察。

《東協經濟數據在中東地緣衝突爆發下仍表現穩健 》
東協主要國家第二季經濟數據整體表現穩健 ，除受
中東地緣風險影響而使通膨壓力有所升溫外 ，其餘
經濟指標普遍較前季改善 。其中，新加坡、印尼及
馬來西亞 GDP成長率更優於市場預期 ，顯示區域景
氣仍具韌性 。若以個別國家觀察 ，印尼與新加坡的
改善範圍最為廣泛 ，即使印尼央行於第二季累計升
息四碼 ，經濟活動仍維持穩健 。然而，貨幣政策對
實體經濟通常存在時滯效應 ，加上第二季數據表現
亮眼墊高基期 ，下半年經濟動能仍面臨一定挑戰 。
值得留意的是 ，印尼第二季固定投資年增 6.9%，創
4季新高，企業資本支出、設備採購及公共建設投資
為主要動能 ，與外國直接投資 (FDI)金額創高相呼應 。
